5
Amenable and Defiant Events

Not all swaps are easy to argue about. Having fixed a client
c
, we
define the
amenable event
and
defiant event
for this
client—the former captures the case where the swaps in
\mathcal{P}
are easy to reason
about, and the latter the case where we throw up our hands and use a
crude bound on the potential change. Thankfully, the latter happens
very rarely, so the loss is small.

Recall that
f_{1}(c),f_{2}(c)
are the two closest local facilities to

c
. Let
f^{*}=f^{*}(c)
be the optimal facility that
c
is assigned to; then

\eta_{1}(f^{*}),\eta_{2}(f^{*})
are the two closest local facilties to

f^{*}
. We define the amenable and defiant events as follows:

Definition 5.1

(Amenable/Defiant)
.

The
defiant event

\mathcal{D}
for a client
c
of type
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
or
\mathsf{E}
is the union of the following events:

(i)

f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, or
\tau(f^{*})
is chosen as a local or optimal surrogate in the degree reduction step;

(ii)

\mathcal{P}
is a tree swaps set, and the out-edge from the original copy of
f^{*}
,
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
is deleted in the edge deletion step.

(iii)

\mathcal{P}
is a simple swaps set, and two connected components each containing a facility in
\{f^{*}\}\cup\{f_{1},f_{2}\}\cup\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
are grouped together in the balancing step.

The
amenable event

\mathcal{A}
is the complement of
\mathcal{D}
.

For type
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
clients, we enlarge the defiant event slightly to include
g^{*}:=\pi(f_{1})
and

g:=\operatorname{argmin}_{h\in F\backslash\{f_{1},f_{2}\}}d(h,g^{*})

as follows:

Definition 5.2

(Amenable/Defiant for type
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
)
.

The
defiant event

\mathcal{D}
for a client
c
of type
\mathsf{C}
or
\mathsf{D}
is the union of the events (i), (ii), (iii) in
Definition

5.1
and the following events:

(i’)

\tau(g^{*})
is chosen as a local or optimal surrogate in the degree reduction step;

(ii’)

\mathcal{P}
is a tree swaps set, and the out-edge from the original copy of
g^{*}
is deleted in the edge deletion step.

(iii’)

\mathcal{P}
is a simple swaps set, and two connected components each containing a facility in
\{f_{1},g\}
are grouped together in the balancing step.

The
amenable event

\mathcal{A}
is the complement of
\mathcal{D}
.

The events
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
depend on the client
c
, but we choose to
omit
c
in our notation because we will always focus on a fixed
client
c
in our proof. We now turn to proving
Lemma

2.2
on
the potential change due to defiant events. The approach is simple: we
first
show a crude upper bound that holds for all swap sets
\mathcal{P}
that we
generate, and then show that the probability of the defiant event is
small enough so that we can afford to apply this crude upper bound.

Claim 5.3
.

There is an absolute constant
\gamma>0
such that for any client
c
, and any swap set
\mathcal{P}
that we generate, we have

\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq\gamma(d^{*}(c)+d_{1}(c))
.

Claim 5.4
.

\Pr[\mathcal{D}]\leq O(\varepsilon)
for all clients
c
.

The proof of
5.4
follows from

3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
,

Corollary

3.8
,
and a trivial union bound. We defer the proof of
5.3
to
Appendix
E.3
.
The two claims above imply
Lemma

2.2
, and hence control the
effect of the defiant events.
We focus next on the amenable
events and the proof of
Lemma

2.3
.

6
The Potential Change due to Amenable Events

Having bounded the potential change due to defiant events, we now turn
to bounding the potential change due to amenable events. Let us recall
the claim we want to
prove:
See
2.3

This section gives an
explicit proof that can be verified by hand. In §
7
we
show how to generate a much larger set of valid inequalities. Solving
the resulting linear program gives improved bounds, but these are more
tedious to verify manually.

6.1
Implications of Amenability

Claim 6.1

(Implications of amenability)
.

For any client, swap sets
\mathcal{P}
generated on the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
have the following properties:

(i)

Any local facility
f\in\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
is closed in at most one swap in
\mathcal{P}
;

(ii)

Any swap in
\mathcal{P}
closing
\tau(f^{*})
must open the original copy of
f^{*}
;

(Tii)

If
\mathcal{P}
is a tree swap set, any swap in
\mathcal{P}
closing
f\in\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
must open
\pi(f)
;

(Siii)

If
\mathcal{P}
is a simple swap set, no swap in
\mathcal{P}
closes two local facilities in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}\cup\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
simultaneously;

(Siv)

If
\mathcal{P}
is a simple swap set, any swap in
\mathcal{P}
closing a local facility in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}\backslash\{\tau(f^{*})\}
does not open
f^{*}
.

For clients of type
\mathsf{C}
or
\mathsf{D}
, we additionally have the following:
(recall that we defined
g^{*}
as
\pi(f_{1})
, and
g
as the local facility closest to
g^{*}
other than
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
):

(ii’)

Any swap in
\mathcal{P}
closing
\tau(g^{*})
must open the original copy of
g^{*}
;

(Siii’)

If
\mathcal{P}
is a simple swap set, no swap in
\mathcal{P}
closes both
f_{1}
and
g
.

Proof of
6.1
.

Recall that the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
is the complement of the defiant event
\mathcal{D}
, defined in
Definition

5.1
.

Implication (i) follows from item (i) of
Definition

5.1
directly.

Implication (ii) follows from items (i) and (ii) of
Definition

5.1
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\tau(f^{*})
is not heavy, since heavy local facilities are never closed. On the amenable event,
\tau(f^{*})
is closed only as its original copy, by item (i) of
Definition

5.1
. The edge to
\tau(f^{*})
from the original copy of
f^{*}
is never deleted by item (ii) of
Definition

5.1
, so the original copies of
f^{*}
and
\tau(f^{*})
must be in the same swap.

Implication (Tii) also follows from items (i) and (ii) of
Definition

5.1
, for a similar reason. Again, assume without loss of generality that neither
f_{1}
nor
f_{2}
is heavy. On the amenable event,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are closed only as their original copies by item (i) of
Definition

5.1
, and the edges
f_{i}\rightarrow\pi(f_{i})
are never deleted by item (ii).

Implications (Siii) and (Siv) both follow from item (iii) of
Definition

5.1
. When we generate the simple swap set, every connected component of the graph
G_{1}
contains at most one local facility, and thus different facilities in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}\cup\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
must be in different connected components, which are not combined in the balancing step due to item (iii) of
Definition

5.1
. This proves implication (Siii). Moreover, the connected component of
f^{*}
doesn’t contain any local facility other than
\tau(f^{*})\in\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
. This proves implication (Siv).

(ii’) and (Siii’) can be proved in the same way as (ii) and (Siii) using
Definition

5.2
.
∎

6.2
Notation and Useful Inequalities

Let
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
denote the expected potential change on client
c

restricted to some generic event
\mathcal{E}
:

{\Delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c):=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)\Big].

Our goal in
Lemma

2.3
is thus to upper bound
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

for the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
. In our proof, we consider sub-events

\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{A}
, and prove worst-case upper-bounds for the potential change
restricted to each sub-event
\mathcal{E}
. Formally, given a suitable
partition
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{E}_{t}
, we define

{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c):=\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)
to be the worst-case
(maximum) value for each event
\mathcal{E}
, and then use:

(6.6)

{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}{\Delta}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}(c)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}\Pr[\mathcal{E}_{i}]\;{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}(c).

For technical reasons, it is more convenient to assume
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
is no smaller than, say,
-10d_{1}(c)
. We thus re-define
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
as
-10d_{1}(c)
when
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)<-10d_{1}(c)
. This doesn’t affect our analysis, as all our upper bounds for
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
are larger than
-10d_{1}(c)
. Also,
5.3
implies that
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)\leq O(d^{*}+d_{1})
.

To apply (
6.6
), we need to understand
\Pr[\mathcal{E}]
and

{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
for the following events (and
their intersections): the
amenable event

\mathcal{A}
and its
complement
defiant event

\mathcal{D}
, the
simple event

\mathcal{S}

and its complement
tree event

\mathcal{T}
. The simple event
\mathcal{S}
is
further partitioned into
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, and the tree event
\mathcal{T}

is partitioned into
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, representing whether

f^{*}
points to
\eta_{1}
or
\eta_{2}
. These events are defined for
a fixed client
c
, and we omit
c
in our notations for brevity.

Recall that
f^{*}
is the optimal facility closest to
c
, and

\rho=\rho(f^{*}):=\frac{d(f^{*},\eta_{1}(f^{*}))}{d(f^{*},\eta_{2}(f^{*}))}
. To
generate the set
\mathcal{P}
of important swaps, we choose
\tau(f^{*})

from
different distributions depending on the value of
\rho(f^{*})
, and
thus the probability of the events
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2}
depends
on
\rho(f^{*})
as follows:

Ratio-types

\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]

\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]

\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]

\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]

0\leq\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

\cdot

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

\cdot

\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

\cdot

\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}

\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq 1

\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho

\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}

Table 1:
Probability distribution for each ratio-type.

Since
\Pr[\mathcal{D}]=O(\varepsilon)
due to
5.4
, the probability of
any event
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{A}
is at least
\Pr[\mathcal{E}]-O(\varepsilon)
.

Bounding the worst-case change
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)
.

We fix an arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated under event
\mathcal{E}
, and
analyze the effect of each swap in
\mathcal{P}
. Let
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
denote the
swap in
\mathcal{P}
that opens
f^{*}
; such a swap always exists. There may
be multiple such swaps in
\mathcal{P}
when we perform tree swaps, in which case we let

{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
be the swap that opens the original copy of
f^{*}
. For a
local facility
f\in\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
, let
{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}
denote the swap
in
\mathcal{P}
that closes
f
. By implication (i) of amenability, there is at most one such
swap as long as
\mathcal{E}
is a sub-event of the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
. When there
is no swap closing
f
(which happens when
f
is a heavy facility), we are often in a better situation because our
bound for
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)
is often non-negative, so we
will mostly focus on the case where
{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}
does exist.

Before we begin giving bounds for the various client types, let us
record in
Table
2
some inequalities we will frequently
use. Recall that
\eta_{1}(f^{*})
and
\eta_{2}(f^{*})
are the closest and
second-closest local facilities to
f^{*}
, and
\pi(f)
is the closest
optimal facility to
f
. These inequalities are proven in

Appendix

C
.

Bound

Conditions
(if any)

d_{2}\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}

(6.7)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}

d(c,\pi(f_{1}))\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}

(6.8)

\max\{d(c,\eta_{1}(f^{*})),d(c,\eta_{2}(f^{*}))\}\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}

(6.9)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}

\min(d^{*},d_{1})+\beta\max(d^{*},d_{1})\leq(1-\beta)\,d^{*}+2\beta\,d_{1}

(6.10)

Table 2:
Useful Inequalities

6.3
Bounds for Clients of Type
\mathsf{E}

We now give an upper bound for the expected potential change
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

for any client
c
of type
\mathsf{E}
. We give the entire proofs here; for
clients of other types we will defer the proofs to the appendices.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any client
c
of type
\mathsf{E}
, we have

{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq 2.5\,d^{*}(c)-0.9\,d_{1}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

In our proof, we partition the amenable event

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A})\cup(\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A})
depending on whether we have a
simple swap or a tree swap, and then bound
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)
by

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}]\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}]\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

(6.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{S}]\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}]\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

The second inequality is implied by
5.4
and our assumption that
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)\geq-10d_{1}
.
To use (
6.11
) we give upper bounds for

{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and
{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
for
clients of both subtypes (close and far) in the next subsections. In
other words, we pick an arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated under these
events, and bound the potential change for client
c
due to the swaps
in
\mathcal{P}
.

6.3.1
Far Clients of Type
\mathsf{E}
:
d_{2}(c)\geq\alpha\,d_{1}(c)

Simple Swaps.

We fix a “far” client
c
and an arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated
conditioned on the event
\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}
for this client, and bound the sum

\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)
.

•

Given the swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\in\mathcal{P}
(which is not
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
by implication (Siv) of amenability),

c
has an additional option of going to
f^{*}
,
giving

{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)\leq(d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}.

•

Next, by implication (i) of amenability, the set
\mathcal{P}
contains at most one
swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
. If
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
does
exist, both
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are open (by implication (Siii) of amenability), and both at distance

\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}
from
c
. Therefore,

{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)\leq(1+\beta)(2d^{*}+d_{1})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}.

This quantity is non-negative: since
c
has type
\mathsf{E}
,
\eta_{1}\neq f
and
also
d(c,\eta_{1})\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}
. But
c
is a far client, then

d(c,\eta_{1})\geq\alpha d_{1}
. Putting the two together:

(1+\beta)(2d^{*}+d_{1})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}\geq d_{1}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}\geq 0.

•

Finally, all other swaps in
\mathcal{P}
leave
f_{1}
open, and thus
they cannot increase the potential for
c
.

Combining these, when the swap
{\langle\!\langle\lnot f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
exists,

(6.12)

\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)+{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)\leq(2+3\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-\beta)\,d_{1}.

In case
{\langle\!\langle\neg{f_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle}
does not exist, (
6.12
)
still holds since our bound for
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
is
non-negative.
Since
\mathcal{P}
was a generic swap set conditioned on being amenable,

{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(2+3\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Tree Swaps.

We now turn to
tree swaps
, and fix an arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}

generated on the event
\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}
. Again,
\mathcal{P}
contains at most one swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}

that closes
f_{1}
, by amenability.
We first consider the case where
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
exists and is the
same as
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
. In this case, all other swaps in
\mathcal{P}
have non-positive potential changes, so

(6.13)

\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)\leq(1+\alpha\beta)d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)d_{1}.

Next, consider the case where
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
. On
swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, client
c
can go to both
d^{*}
and
d_{1}
. On swap

{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
c
can go to
\pi(f_{1})
at distance
\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
, and
also to
\tau(f^{*})\in\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
at distance
\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}
. Both these facilities
\pi(f_{1})
and
\tau(f^{*})
must be open
after the swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
due to implications (ii) and (Sii) of amenabilityx. All other swaps in
\mathcal{P}
have non-positive potential changes, so

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)+{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle+(2d^{*}+d_{1})+\beta(2d_{1}+d^{*})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(6.14)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(2+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{1}.

In the case where
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
doesn’t exist,
(
6.14
) still holds, because our bound for

{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}(c)
is non-negative. By our choice of
\alpha=3

and
\beta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{5}}
, (
6.13
) is dominated by
(
6.14
). Since
\mathcal{P}
is a generic swap set,

{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(2+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Summarizing the simple swaps case and the tree swaps case, we have

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(2+3\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-\beta)\,d_{1}\leq 2.6\,d^{*}-d_{1},

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(2+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{1}\leq 2.4\,d^{*}-0.8\,d_{1}.

Now substituting into (
6.11
), we get a bound for all type
\mathsf{E}
far clients
c
:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(6.15)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.5\,d^{*}-0.9\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

This proves
Lemma

6.2
for far clients of type
\mathsf{E}
.
The proof for all other types of clients will have a similar
structure: we will identify which swaps affect client
c
, then we sum
up the inequalities with the right probabilities. In some cases we
will need to look at cases depending on
\rho
.

6.3.2
Close Clients of Type
\mathsf{E}
:
d_{2}(c)\geq\alpha\,d_{1}(c)

Simple swaps.
Now we consider the case of close clients
c
. We fix an arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}
, and
focus on
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, and
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
(All
other swaps cause a non-positive potential change). Suppose these
three swaps are different. When
f^{*}
opens, the client
c
can be
served by both
f^{*}
and
f_{1}
. When
f_{1}
closes,
c
can be served
by
f_{2}
and
\eta_{1}
, and when
f_{2}
closes,
c
can be served by

f_{1}
and
\eta_{1}
: in both these cases, we use implication (Siii) of amenability
to ensure that both the corresponding facilities are open. We know that

d_{2}\leq d(c,\eta_{1})
because
c
has type
\mathsf{E}
; by (
2
)
we get
d(c,\eta_{1})\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}
. Putting everything together,
the three swaps yield:

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d^{*}+\beta\,d_{1}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle+d_{2}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle+d_{1}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=\boxed{(1+4\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-3\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-3\beta)\,d_{2}}.

We address the assumption that the three swaps are different. As
argued above, condition (Siv) of amenability for type
\mathsf{E}
clients means that for simple swaps,

{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
. However,
f_{2}
could be
\tau(f^{*})
, so it may happen that

{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, and hence that

{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}+{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
. Moreover,
{\langle\!\langle\lnot f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
may not
exist, in which case
{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}+{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
or even
{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
. But since our bounds above
for both
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
and
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}

are non-negative, we infer that
the boxed upper bound
remains
valid in all these cases.

Tree swaps.
We now consider tree swaps. Fix an
arbitrary swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated on the event
\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}
. For a
client
c
in the close case, there are three swaps that are relevant
to
c
—those containing
f^{*}
,
f_{1}
, and
f_{2}
—although some of these
swaps may coincide. (Also, no other swaps can increase the potential.)

When
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
belong to the same swap.

First suppose that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
belong to the same swap in
\mathcal{P}
. We start from the case where
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
. For the swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client
c
can be served by both
f^{*}

and
f_{1}
. And when
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both closed,
c
can be served
by
\tau(f^{*})
(which is either
\eta_{1}
or
\eta_{2}
) and

\pi(f_{1})
. By (
2
) we get that
d(c,\tau(f^{*}))
is at most

2d^{*}+d_{1}
, and by (
2
) we get
d(c,\pi(f_{1}))\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)

\displaystyle\leq{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}+{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\leq(d^{*}+\beta\,d_{1})-(d_{1}+\beta\,d_{2})

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~~+(2d^{*}+d_{1})+\beta(2d_{1}+d^{*})-(d_{1}+\beta\,d_{2})

\displaystyle=\boxed{(3+\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-3\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}}.

On the other hand, if
f^{*},f_{1}
, and
f_{2}
all belong to the same
swap, we can assign
c
to
f^{*}

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(since
2d^{*}+d_{1}\geq d_{2}
)

\displaystyle+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1}-d_{2})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 3.2\,d^{*}-0.4\,d_{1}-0.4\,d_{2}.

These two bounds are identical for our choices of
\alpha=3
and

\beta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{5}}
.

When
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
belong to different swaps.

Next, consider the case when
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
belong to
different swaps in
\mathcal{P}
. Let us first assume
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
is neither
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
nor
{\langle\!\langle f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
. In the swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
the client can served by

f^{*}
and
f_{1}
. When one of
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
is closed, the client
c

can be served by the other facility, and by
\tau(f^{*})
, which is at
distance at most
2d^{*}+d_{1}
from
c
(by (
2
)). Hence,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
with (
2
))

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\beta)\,d^{*}+2\beta\,d_{1}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+d_{2}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+d_{1}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=\boxed{(1+3\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-4\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-3\beta)\,d_{2}}.

Our bound for
{\delta}_{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
does not require
f_{2}
to remain open after the swap, and our bound for
{\delta}_{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
is non-negative. Therefore, the above bound also holds when
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
. When
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, we still have the above bound:

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}\delta_{(P,Q)}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d^{*}+\beta\,d_{2}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+d_{1}+\beta(2d^{*}+d_{1})-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(non-negative terms)

\displaystyle~~~+\beta\,d^{*}+\beta\,d_{1}+(1-2\beta)(d_{2}-d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{(1+3\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-4\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-3\beta)\,d_{2}}.

Summarizing all these
bounds (using that
\alpha=3
and
\beta=0.2
),

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1+4\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-3\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-3\beta)\,d_{2}=1.8\,d^{*}-1.4\,d_{1}+0.4\,d_{2}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{3.2\,d^{*}-0.4\,d_{1}-0.4\,d_{2},1.6\,d^{*}-1.2\,d_{1}+0.4\,d_{2}\}.

Combining and using (
2
) to get
d_{2}\leq 2d^{*}+d_{1}
if the
d_{2}
terms do not cancel out, we get for close clients
c
:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(6.16)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.5\,d^{*}-0.9\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

Lemma

6.2
follows from the bound in (
6.15
) for the far clients and the
one from (
6.16
) for the close clients.

6.4
All Other Client Types

Similarly, we can bound
\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})
for every other client type

\mathsf{A}
–
\mathsf{D}
. We summarize this in the following theorem: the calculations
behind the expressions can be found in
Appendix

D
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any far client
c
of type
\mathsf{A}
or
\mathsf{B}
, we have

(6.17)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})\leq 2.47\,d^{*}(c)-1.13\,d_{1}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

For any close client
c_{i}
of type
i\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}\}
, we have

(6.18)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c_{\mathsf{A}}}){}

\displaystyle\leq 2.375\,d^{*}(c_{\mathsf{A}})-0.9\,d_{1}(c_{\mathsf{A}})+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(6.19)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c_{\mathsf{B}}}){}

\displaystyle\leq 2.4\,d^{*}(c_{\mathsf{B}})-0.9\,d_{1}(c_{\mathsf{B}})+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(6.20)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c_{\mathsf{C}}}){}

\displaystyle\leq 2.2\,d^{*}(c_{\mathsf{C}})-0.8888\,d_{1}(c_{\mathsf{C}})+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(6.21)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c_{\mathsf{D}}}){}

\displaystyle\leq 2.5203\,d^{*}(c_{\mathsf{D}})-0.8888\,d_{1}(c_{\mathsf{D}})+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

Lemmas

6.2
and
6.3
imply that every client
c
satisfies

\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})\leq 2.5203\,d^{*}(c)-0.8888\,d_{1}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

This proves

Lemma

2.3
, and hence
Lemma

2.1
and Theorem
1.1
.

7
A Computer-Aided Analysis using Linear Programming

In this section we show how to generate a set of valid inequalities,
then solve the resulting linear program to find an upper bound on our
approximation ratio. We describe the ideas for the potential
\Phi_{2}

that only takes the second-closest facility into account, and indicate
how to extend it to
\Phi_{q}
for higher values of
q\geq 2
. Of course, the
size of the LP increases exponentially as
q
increases.

To recall, our proof strategy in the previous section was to consider
a local optimum, and then:

1.

define a (randomized) collection of important swaps that are contained
within our actual set of swaps;

2.

for every client type, write constraints that apply to all clients of that type;

3.

carefully combine those constraints to have only a few remaining
constraints; and

4.

manually check these remaining contraints.

An automated proof could avoid the last two steps by directly checking
the entire set of constraints. Since every constraint we derive is a
linear inequality on the distances, a linear program can be used for
this automated proof.
Put differently, our goal is to write a linear program that constructs
a “worst-case example” for our potential function. Specifically, the
program seeks values of the distances
d_{1},d_{2}
, and
d^{*}
for each
client type, so as to maximize the ratio between the costs of the
optimum and local solutions, while respecting the set of constraints.

1
1

1

In fact, it does not come up with a concrete example, since we do not
maintain all the triangle inequalities between the clients, but only
the triangle inequalities in some local neighborhood around each
client. It is conceivable that using more triangle inequalities would
lead to an even better result, but that increases the complexity even further.

Variables and constraints of the LP.

Let us focus on simple swaps, the constraints for tree swaps are
similar. We want to express the fact that simple swaps at a local
optimum do not decrease the potential. We first classify facilities into
types according to their ratio
\rho
; we consider only a fine net of
values for
\rho
, and use continuity of the potential to control the
loss due to this discretization. All facilities with a given ratio are
treated the same way in the proof: our LP considers that all facilities
of the same type are swapped at the same time. Specifically, we have
a variable
s_{\rho}
corresponding to the difference in the potential
function after applying simple swaps for all facilities with ratio

\rho
. The constraint saying that simple swaps do not decrease the
potential is therefore
\sum_{\rho}s_{\rho}\geq 0
.

The value of the variable
s_{\rho}
is controlled by the clients
connected to facilities having ratio
\rho
: each client type
i
has a
contribution to it. In an
S_{j}
-swap (where
j\in\{1,2\}
), let

\delta_{S_{j}}(i;x,y^{1},y^{2})
be the potential change due to all clients of
type-
i
connected to facilities with ratio
\rho
, in function of
x,y^{1}
and
y^{2}
, respectively the total distance from those clients to the optimal solution, their closest and their second closest facility of the local solution. This difference of
potential is described in Section
6
: we illustrate it
with clients of type
\mathsf{A}
, in the far case. We denote
\mathsf{AF}
those
clients. As presented in
D.1.1
, the
S_{1}
and

S_{2}
swaps for those clients show

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,d^{*}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

For bounding
\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
, we upper bounded the potential value
of the swap by
d^{*}+\beta d_{1}
when
f^{*}
is opened. However, we could be more precise:
it could be the case that
d^{*}\leq d_{1}
or
d_{1}\leq\alpha d^{*}
. Therefore, this lead to 3 other possible upperbounds, namely
(1+\alpha\beta)d^{*}
,
d^{*}+\beta d_{1}
, and
(1+\alpha\beta)d_{1}
.

This translates to three other inequalities, one for each of those cases:

(when we choose
(1+\alpha\beta)d^{*}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq((2+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,d^{*}-((2-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,d_{1}

(when we choose
d^{*}+\beta d_{1}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+1)\,d^{*}-((2-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)-\beta)\,d_{1}

(when we choose
(1+\alpha\beta)d_{1}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,d^{*}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,d_{1}.

More generally, the LP encodes all possible combinations of variables giving valid bound on the potential after a swap. Note that then number of such inequalities grows exponentially with
q
, because each term
\min(\alpha_{j}d_{1}(c),d_{j}(c))
doubles the number of valid inequalities.

Going back to type
\mathsf{A}
, this gives rise to the constraints

(7.22)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{1}}(\mathsf{AF};x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha\beta)x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}-(1+\alpha\beta)y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1}

(7.23)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{2}}(\mathsf{AF};x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1}

(7.24)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{2}}(\mathsf{AF};x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq((2+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}-((2-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1}

(7.25)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{2}}(\mathsf{AF};x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+1)\,x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}-((2-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)-\beta)\,y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1}

(7.26)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{2}}(\mathsf{AF};x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta))\,y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1},

where the variables
x_{i,\rho}
denote the total cost of clients of
type
i
connected to facilities with ratio
\rho
in the optimal
solution, and
y_{i,\rho}^{j}
denote the total distance from those
clients to their
j
-th closest facility in the local solution.
This definition of
\delta_{S_{j}}(i)
yields the following constraint on
s_{\rho}
: for
j=1,2
,

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{ST}}\delta_{S_{j}}(i;x_{i,\rho},y_{i,\rho}^{1},y_{i,\rho}^{2})\geq s_{\rho},

where
\mathcal{ST}
is the set of
client types.

Moreover, the triangle inequality gives constraints on the variables

x_{i,\rho},y_{i,\rho}^{1},y_{i,\rho}^{2}
: for instance, for type

\mathsf{AF}
, we would have

(7.27)

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{2}\leq x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(\leq x_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}+y_{\mathsf{AF},\rho}^{1}).

The constraints due to tree swaps are defined analogously, with a
variable
t_{\rho}
being the potential change after applying tree swaps
for all facilities of type
\rho
, and
\delta_{T_{j}}(i)
being the
potential change due to all clients of type-
i
connected to facilities
with ratio
\rho
.
For
q>2
, we need to consider more than one ratio, so we let
\rho
be
the vector of size
q-1
that describes ratio of all two consecutive
\eta_{j}
and
\eta_{j+1}
for all
j\in\{1,...,q-1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of values of
\rho
after
discretization: we use
\mathcal{R}:=\{\frac{i}{100}\mid i\in\{0,\ldots,100\}\}^{q-1}
. In that case, all clients with ratio in
[i\cdot 10^{-2},(i+1)\cdot 10^{-2})
are considered to have
\rho=i\cdot 10^{-2}
for each index.
This means that our bounds for
\delta
are slightly relaxed to cover an interval instead of a precise
\rho
.
The
j^{th}
index of a ratio correspond to
\frac{d(f^{*}(c),\eta_{j}(f^{*}(c)))}{d(f^{*}(c),\eta_{j-1}(f^{*}(c)))}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of client types. For
q=2
, each
i\in\mathcal{C}
contains
client type
(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D},\mathsf{E})
, ratio
\rho
, underlying form of tree-graph (e.g.
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
belong to same tree in
\eta_{1}
-swap),
and whether
d_{2}\leq\alpha d_{1}
or not.
For
j\in\{2,...,q\}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{j}
be the set of clients with
d_{j}\leq\alpha_{j}d_{1}
.
For
q=2
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is the set of close clients (i.e.,
d_{2}\leq\alpha d_{1}
).
Let
C^{\rho}
denote set of clients with ratio
\rho
.
The general structure of the LP is the following:

(7.28)

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}}y^{1}_{i}

(7.29)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}}x_{i}=1

(7.30)

\displaystyle y^{j}_{i}\leq\alpha_{j}y^{1}_{i}

\displaystyle\forall j\in\{2,\ldots,q\},i\in\mathcal{C}_{j}

(7.31)

\displaystyle y^{j}_{i}\geq\alpha_{j}y^{1}_{i}

\displaystyle\forall j\in\{2,\ldots,q\},i\not\in\mathcal{C}_{j}

(7.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}^{\rho}}\delta_{S_{j}}(i;x_{i},y_{i}^{1},\ldots,y_{i}^{q})\geq s_{\rho}

\displaystyle\forall\rho\in\mathcal{R},j\in\{1,\ldots,q\}

(7.33)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}^{\rho}}\delta_{T_{j}}(i;x_{i},y_{i}^{1},\ldots,y_{i}^{q})\geq t_{\rho}

\displaystyle\forall\rho\in\mathcal{R},j\in\{1,\ldots,q\}

(7.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{R}}s_{\rho}\geq 0

(7.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{R}}t_{\rho}\geq 0

(7.36)

Triangle-inequalities

(7.37)

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{j}}(i;x_{i},y_{i}^{1},\ldots,y_{i}^{q})\leq\text{enumerated-upperbounds}

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{C},j\in\{2,...,q\}

(7.38)

\displaystyle\delta_{T_{j}}(i;x_{i},y_{i}^{1},\ldots,y_{i}^{q})\leq\text{enumerated-upperbounds}

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{C},j\in\{2,...,q\}

(7.39)

\displaystyle y^{j}_{i}\geq 0,\quad x_{i}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{C},j\in\{1,\ldots,q\}

Note that
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}}y^{1}_{i}/\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}}x_{i}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{C}}y^{1}_{i}
is the locality gap.
Constraints (
7.30
) and (
7.31
) restrict each distance based on whether they are ‘far’ client or ‘close’ client.
Constraints (
7.32
) can be seen as the following: for each ratio
\rho

we pick
j\in\{1,...,q\}
that minimizes the sum of potential difference
after performing
S_{j}
swap, then make
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{j}
for all
f^{*}
with ratio
\rho
. Similarly, (
7.33
) chooses
\tau(\cdot)

for tree swaps. Then (
7.34
) and (
7.35
) ensure
the potential difference is non-negative after performing simple swap and tree swap respectively.
We also add triangle inequalities (e.g., (
7.27
)). Lastly, we add upperbounds
for each potential difference in
(
7.37
) and (
7.38
) (e.g., (
7.22
) - (
7.26
)).

Implementing this approach, and then
solving the resulting LP for for potential
\Phi_{2}
and
\Phi_{3}
gives
us the following numbers:

Potential

Bound

\Phi_{2}

2.7786

\Phi_{3}

2.6861

For
\Phi_{2}
, the LP finds that taking
\alpha=3,\beta=0.2
yields the best result, whereas for

\Phi_{3}
we set manually

\alpha=2.5,\beta=0.3,\beta_{2}=\beta\cdot 0.34
.
As always, we get an additive
\varepsilon
term because of the defiant
swaps. However, let us emphasize that these implementations should be considered
preliminary, since they have not been formally verified. We hope that
formal proofs of these results can be given in the near future.

Appendix A
Locality Gap for Potential
\Phi_{2}

In this section, we give lower bounds on the locality gap, and prove
Theorem
1.2
. We show locality gap examples of
\max\{2,\alpha\}
,

3-2\beta
, and
1+4\beta
for the potential function
\Phi_{2}
. Putting these
together, the locality gap is

\min_{\beta\in[0,1],\alpha\in[1,\infty)}\max\{3-2\beta,1+4\beta\},\max\{2,\alpha\}
.
Note
\max\{3-2\beta,1+4\beta\}
is
2\frac{1}{3}
, when
we set
\beta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
. Therefore we show a locality gap of
2
.

In this section, we show a locality gap of
2
for
\Phi_{2}
.
We divide the cases into three main cases:

\bullet

When
\alpha\leq 2

\bullet

When
\alpha>2
and
\beta\leq 1/3

\bullet

When
\alpha>2
and
\beta>1/3

We mainly use two types of example that we call
“bi-clique” and “double-bi-clique” described in
Figure

A.9

Figure

A.10
respectively.
In bi-clique we have
k+r
local facilities on the right, where
r=O(1)
is the number of extra local facilities, and

k
optimal facilities are on the left. There is a client between
every (local, optimal) facility pair, at unit distance from the
optimal facility, and at distance
d
from the local
facility.

\vdots
\vdots
1
d

Figure A.9:
An illustration of the bi-clique example with
r=0
.

In double-bi-clique, we have two back-to-back bi-cliques as in
Figure

A.10
.
Each bi-clique is constructed the same way
as Figure
A.9
except the number of facilities are halved.
Consider a client
c
with an edge going into
f_{i}
,
create an edge at distance
d
between
c
and
i^{th}
local facility in the
other bi-clique.
Now every client has an optimal facility at distance 1,
and two local facilities at distance
d
.

1
d
d
1
d
d

Figure A.10:
The double-bi-clique example with
k=4
and
r=0
.

For all cases we calculate the potential difference
after performing a swap of size
p
.
There are mainly 4 different types of clients.

\bullet

\mathcal{C}_{o}
: the set of clients with their
f^{*}
opened.

\bullet

\mathcal{C}_{1}
: Clients with their
f_{1}
closed and
f^{*}
not opened

\bullet

\mathcal{C}_{2}
: Clients with its
f_{2}
closed and
f^{*}
not opened

\bullet

\mathcal{C}_{3}
: Clients with its
f^{*}
closed,
f_{1}
opened, and
f_{2}
opened.

We use
c_{o}
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
c_{3}
to denote generic
client for sets
\mathcal{C}_{o}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}

respectively. We first calculate potential difference
for each client type, then sum them over.

We assume there is no client with their
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
both closed:
those clients can only hurt the quality of the
solution, and given a swap that closes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of some clients it is easy to construct a strictly better set of swaps with no such client.

A.1
When
\alpha\leq 2

We first give lower bound examples when
\alpha\leq 2
.
We divide the case further into two cases:
when
\alpha\leq 4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}}
and when
\alpha>4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}}
.

Subcase I:
\alpha\leq 4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}}
.

We create a bi-clique presented in
Figure

A.9
with
d=2-\varepsilon^{\prime}
, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}\approx O(1/k)
is a small quantity to be specified later.
Note that every client has
k+r-1
local facilities at distance
2+d
,
thus the second closest facility is never closed for any client.
Then for each client we get the potential differences:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}

\displaystyle=1+\alpha\beta-(2-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(2-\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha\beta=-1-\alpha\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}+\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=(4-\varepsilon^{\prime})+(4-\varepsilon^{\prime})\beta-(2-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(2-\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha\beta\geq 2+4\beta-2\alpha\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{3}}

\displaystyle=0

Note
|\mathcal{C}_{o}|=p(k+r)
and
|\mathcal{C}_{1}|=p(k-p)
.

Summing up gives

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}

\displaystyle\geq p(k+r)(-1-\alpha\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}+\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\beta)+p(k-p)(2+4\beta-2\alpha\beta)

\displaystyle\geq pk(1+4\beta-3\alpha\beta)+pk\varepsilon^{\prime}+rp(-1-\alpha\beta)-p^{2}(2+4\beta-2\alpha\beta)

(for
\alpha\leq 4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}}
)

\displaystyle\geq pk(1+4\beta-3\alpha\beta)\geq 0

The second inequality holds for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\frac{p^{2}(2+4\beta-2\alpha\beta)+rp(1+\alpha\beta)}{pk}=O(1/k)
. Hence, this example shows a locality gap of
2-o(1)
when
\alpha\leq\min(2,4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}})
.

Subcase II:
\alpha>4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{(3\beta)}}
.

Since we focus on
\alpha\leq 2
and
\beta\leq 1
, this subcase implies that
\beta>1/2
and
\alpha>5/3
.
To deal with it, we create a double-bi-clique presented in
Figure

A.10
with
d=2
.
Note that every client has two local facilities at distance
2
, and
k+r-2
facilities at distance

4
.
Then for each client, we get the following potential differences:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}

\displaystyle=1+\alpha\beta-2-2\beta=-1+\alpha\beta-2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=2+2\alpha\beta-2-2\beta\geq 2\alpha\beta-2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=2+2\alpha\beta-2-2\beta\geq 2\alpha\beta-2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{3}}

\displaystyle=0

Let
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
be the number of optimal facilities in the first clique that belong to the swap.
Let
p_{2}
be the number of optimal facilities in the second clique that belong to the swap.
Then we have
|\mathcal{C}_{o}|=\frac{k+r}{2}p_{1}+\frac{k+r}{2}p_{2}=\frac{k+r}{2}p
.
Similarly let
p^{\prime}_{1}
and
p^{\prime}_{2}
be the number of local facilities in the first and second clique that belong to the swap. Then we have
|\mathcal{C}_{1}|=p^{\prime}_{1}(\frac{k}{2}-p_{1})+p^{\prime}_{2}(\frac{k}{2}-p_{2})\geq(\frac{k}{2}-p)p
. Also note that
|\mathcal{C}_{2}|\geq(\frac{k}{2}-p)p
.
Summing up gives

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}

\displaystyle\geq\nicefrac{{p}}{{2}}(k+r)(-1+\alpha\beta-2\beta)+2p(\nicefrac{{k}}{{2}}-p)(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)

\displaystyle\geq p\nicefrac{{k}}{{2}}(-1+5\alpha\beta-6\beta)-\nicefrac{{rp}}{{2}}(1+2\beta-\alpha\beta)-2p^{2}(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)

(for
\alpha>5/3
and
\beta>1/2
.)

\displaystyle\geq pk(1/12)-\nicefrac{{r}}{{2}}p(1+2\beta-\alpha\beta)-2p^{2}(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)\geq 0

The last inequality holds for
r\leq\frac{k}{24(1+2\beta-\alpha\beta)}
and
p\leq\frac{k}{48(2\alpha\beta-2\beta)}
.
Since
r
and
p
are absolute constant (i.e,
o(k)
), the inequality is valid for big enough
k
. Hence, this example shows a locality gap of
2
when
4/3+\nicefrac{{1}}{{3\beta}}\leq\alpha\leq 2
, in particular when
\beta>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
2\geq\alpha\geq\nicefrac{{5}}{{3}}
This concludes therefore the case
\alpha\leq 2
.

A.2
When
\beta\leq 1/2
and
\alpha>2

In this section we give a bi-clique example showing a locality
gap when
\beta\leq 1/3
and
\alpha>2
. for constant-sized swap.
Consider the bi-clique graph in
Figure

A.9

with distance
d=\min\{3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime},\alpha\}
.
We divide the case into two subcases.
In first case we consider when
3-2\beta\leq\alpha
.
Then we consider when
3-2\beta>\alpha
.

Subcase I:
3-2\beta\leq\alpha
.

We will first consider the case when
3-2\beta\leq\alpha
. In that case, the current potential value of a client in the local solution is

\Phi^{c}(F)=(3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})+\beta(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})
(since
\alpha>2\geq\frac{5-2\beta}{3-2\beta}
for
\beta\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
).

For the
p(k+r)
clients in
\mathcal{C}_{o}
, where
k+r
is the number of local facilities, if
3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\alpha
we get the following potential difference:

\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}\geq 1+\beta(3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-\beta(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})=-2+\varepsilon^{\prime}

Note that if a client’s
f^{*}
is opened but its
f_{1}
is closed, the client contributes
1+\beta(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})\geq 1+\beta(3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})
, and hence the above inequality is still valid for those clients.

There are
p(k-p)
clients in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, and they induce the following potential difference:

\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}=(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})+\beta(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-\beta(5-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})=2

Finally, clients in
\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
\mathcal{C}_{4}
do not induce a change in the potential value.

The sum over all clients yields

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}

\displaystyle\geq p(k+r)(\varepsilon^{\prime}-2)+2p(k-p)

\displaystyle=pk(0)+p(k+r)(\varepsilon^{\prime})-2(pr)-2p^{2}\geq 0.

The last inequality holds for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\frac{2pr+2p^{2}}{p(k+r)}=O(1/k)
.

Hence, in the case where
\alpha\geq 2,~\beta\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
3-2\beta\leq\alpha
, this example shows a locality gap of
3-2\beta-o(1)\geq 2
.

Case II:
2\leq\alpha\leq 3-2\beta
.

When
2\leq\alpha\leq 3-2\beta
, client’s closest distance is now
\alpha
.
Thus the potential value of a client before any swap is
\Phi^{c}(F)=\alpha+(2+\alpha)\beta
. Note that
2+\alpha\leq\alpha^{2}
for
\alpha\geq 2
.
We have the same number of clients in each set. Furthermore, we get the potential differences:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}

\displaystyle\geq 1+\alpha\beta-\alpha-(2+\alpha)\beta\geq 1-\alpha-2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=(2+\alpha)+(2+\alpha)\beta-\alpha-(2+\alpha)\beta=2

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{3}}

\displaystyle=0

The sum over all clients yields

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}

\displaystyle\geq p(k+r)(1-\alpha-2\beta)+p(k-p)(2)

\displaystyle\geq pk(3-2\beta-\alpha)+pr(1-\alpha-2\beta)-2p^{2}

(
\alpha\leq 3-2\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\geq pk\varepsilon^{\prime}-pr(\alpha+2\beta-1)-2p^{2}\geq 0.

The last inequality holds for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\frac{pr(\alpha-1-2\beta)+2p^{2}}{pk}=O(1/k)
.

Hence, in the case where
\alpha\leq 3-2\beta
,
\alpha\geq 2
and
\beta\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
, this example shows a locality gap of
\alpha\geq 2
.

A.3
When
\beta>{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
and
\alpha>2

Finally we give lower bound examples when
\beta>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
\alpha>2
.
We use double-bi-clique in described
Figure

A.10
with
d=\min\{1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime},\alpha\}
.

Subcase I:
1+4\beta\leq\alpha
.

Here, the current potential function value for a client is
\Phi^{c}(F)=(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})+\beta(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})
.

There are
p(\frac{k+r}{2})
clients in
\mathcal{C}_{o}
, and the potential difference for a client
c_{o}\in\mathcal{C}_{o}
is

\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}=1+\beta(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(1+\beta)(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})=-4\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}.

There are at least
p(\frac{k}{2}-p)
clients in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Recall
\alpha>2\geq\frac{3+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime}}{1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime}}
for
\beta>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. The potential difference for
c_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
is

\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}=(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})+\beta(3+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})-(1+\beta)(1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime})=2\beta

There are
p(\frac{k}{2}-p)
clients in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and they get the same swap value as clients in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
Clients in
\mathcal{C}_{3}
do not induce any change in the potential.

Then sum over all clients yields

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}\geq-4\beta p\left(\frac{k+r}{2}\right)+\varepsilon^{\prime}p\left(\frac{k+r}{2}\right)+4\beta\left(\frac{pk}{2}-p^{2}\right)=\varepsilon^{\prime}\left(\frac{p(k+r)}{2}\right)-4\beta p\left(p+\nicefrac{{r}}{{2}}\right)\geq 0

The difference in potential function is therefore positive for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}>\frac{4\beta r+8\beta p}{(k+r)}=O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)
. Hence, this example shows a locality gap of
1+4\beta-o(1)\geq 2-o(1)
when
\alpha\geq\max(2,1+4\beta)
and
\beta\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

Case II:
2\leq\alpha\leq 1+4\beta
.

When
\alpha\leq 1+4\beta
, clients’ closest and the second closest local facilities are both at distance
\alpha
.
Thus the current potential value for a client is
\Phi^{c}(F)=\alpha+\alpha\beta
.
We have the same number of clients in each set. We get the following potential differences:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{o}}

\displaystyle=1+\alpha\beta-\alpha-\alpha\beta\geq 1-\alpha

(Note
2\leq\alpha
implies
(2+\alpha)\leq\alpha^{2}
.)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=\alpha+(2+\alpha)\beta-\alpha-\alpha\beta=2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=\alpha+(2+\alpha)\beta-\alpha-\alpha\beta=2\beta

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi^{c_{3}}

\displaystyle=0

The sum over all clients yields

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\Delta\Phi^{c}

\displaystyle\geq p\left(\frac{k+r}{2}\right)(1-\alpha)+2(\nicefrac{{pk}}{{2}}-p^{2})(2\beta)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{pk}{2}(1-\alpha+4\beta)-\frac{pr}{2}(\alpha-1)-2p^{2}(2\beta)

(
\alpha\leq 1+4\beta-\varepsilon^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{pk}{2}(\varepsilon^{\prime})-\frac{pr}{2}(\alpha-1)-2p^{2}(2\beta)\geq 0

The last inequality holds for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\frac{pr(\alpha-1)+8p^{2}\beta}{pk}=O(1/k)
.
Hence, this example shows a locality gap of
\alpha-o(1)\geq 2-o(1)
when
2\leq\alpha\leq 1+4\beta
and
\beta\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

Appendix B
Motivating our Swaps

In this section we present examples that motivate our choice of
potential function and our swaps. In particular they show that the
swap structures defined in previous works are not powerful enough to
prove our results.

Figure B.11:
A bad scenario for the swap structures defined
by
[
GT08
]
.

The analysis in
[
GT08
]
matches each optimal facility to its
closest local facility. So it matches both OPT1, OPT2 to ALG2 and both OPT3, OPT4 to
ALG4, leaving ALG1 and ALG3 with no facility of
\mathsf{opt}
matched to
them. Hence, two swaps are defined: (1) swapping in OPT1 and OPT2 and
removing ALG2 and ALG
j
for some
j\in\{1,3\}
that remains
unspecified in their analysis, and (2) swapping in OPT3 and OPT4 and
removing ALG4 and ALG(4-
j
). Now, if we consider the swaps defined
by choosing
j=3
, the set of equations obtained does not allow us
to deduce that the solution is not a local optimum, as long as

\alpha>5/3
and for any
\beta<1
.

Figure B.12:
A bad scenario for the swap structures defined by Arya et al.
[
AGK+01
]
.

The definition of the swap structure in
[
AGK+01
]

does not uniquely identify which local facility is matched to which
optimal facility. Hence, if the analysis matches ALG1 with OPT4, ALG2
with OPT3, ALG3 with OPT2 and ALG4 with OPT1, the set of linear
equations obtained does not allow us to deduce that the instance is not a
local optimum.

Appendix C
Useful Inequalities

In this section, we prove the inequalities in Table
2
.
We also give some more inequalities in Table
3
;
these will be used in §
D
.

Bound

Coundition

d_{2}\geq(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}-(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d^{*}

(C.40)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})=f_{1}

d_{2}\leq d^{*}+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(d_{1}+d^{*})

(C.41)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})=f_{1}

d(c,\eta_{2}(f^{*}))\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}(d^{*}+d_{1})

(C.42)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})=f_{1}

(C.43)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}

(C.44)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}

(C.45)

(C.46)

\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}

Table 3:
More useful inequalities

For clients
c
with
\eta_{1}(f^{*})=f_{1}
:

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\textstyle d(f^{*},\eta_{2})-d^{*}=\frac{1}{\rho}d(f^{*},\eta_{1})-d^{*}\geq(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}-(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d^{*},

(proving (
3
))

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq d^{*}+d(f^{*},\eta_{2})=d^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho}d(f^{*},\eta_{1})\leq d^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho}(d_{1}+d^{*}).

(proving (
3
))

\displaystyle d(c,\eta_{2})

\displaystyle\leq d(c,f^{*})+d(f^{*},\eta_{2})=d^{*}+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})d(f^{*},f_{1})\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}(d^{*}+d_{1}).

(proving (
3
))

Else when
\eta_{1}(f^{*})\neq f_{1}
:

\displaystyle d(c,\eta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq d(c,f^{*})+d(f^{*},\eta_{1})=d(c,f^{*})+\rho\,d(f^{*},\eta_{2})\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})

(proving (
3
))

\displaystyle d(c,\eta_{2})

\displaystyle\leq d(c,f^{*})+d(f^{*},\eta_{2})\leq d^{*}+(d^{*}+d_{1}).

(proving (
3
))

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle\leq d(c,\eta_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:apx:eta1}}}{{\leq}}d^{*}+\rho(d_{1}+d^{*}),

(proving
(
3
) and (
2
))

Combining (
3
) and (
3
) gives (
2
).

Recalling that
\pi(f)
is the closest optimal facility to
f
, we get
for any client
c
,

\displaystyle d(c,\pi(f_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq d(c,f_{1})+d(f_{1},\pi(f_{1}))\leq d_{1}+d(f_{1},f^{*})\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}

(proving (
2
))

\displaystyle d(c,\pi(f_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq d(c,f_{2})+d(f_{2},\pi(f_{2}))\leq d_{2}+d(f_{2},f^{*})\leq 2d_{2}+d^{*}.

(proving (
3
))

To prove (
2
), we use that for any
a,b\geq 0
and

\beta\leq 1
, the expression

\min(a,b)+\beta\,\max(a,b)=\min(a+\beta b,b+\beta a)
is smaller
than any convex combination

(1-\lambda)(a+\beta b)+\lambda(b+\beta a)
with
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Setting

\lambda=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}
and simplifying gives
(1-\beta)a+2\beta b
. Using
a=d^{*}
and
b=d_{1}
completes the proof.

Appendix D
Proof of
Lemma

6.3

We now present the proof of
Lemma

6.3
, giving bounds for
all the client types other than type
\mathsf{E}
. The idea is the same for
each one: First we fix a client
c
of some type. We partition the
amenable event into some sub-events, and look on some sub-event
\mathcal{E}
.
We consider a generic swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated under that event, and
give an upper bound for the maximum potential change for client
c

due to these swaps. Combining over all sub-events (with the correct
probability values) gives the expected potential change. The largest
such change for each client type is then shown to be the one recorded
in
Lemma

6.3
.

When we prove upper bounds for the potential change caused by a swap set
\mathcal{P}
,
we assume that both
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
and
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
exist in
\mathcal{P}

(if
f
is heavy
{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}
does not exist).
As we mentioned in
Section

6.2
, our bounds also hold in
cases where either of them does not exist, because our upper bounds for

{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}}
is non-negative as long as
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f\rangle\!\rangle}
for
any
f\in\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
.

In the rest of this section, we prove each
inequality from
Lemma

6.3
.

See
6.3

D.1
Proof of (
6.17
): Far Clients of Type
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}

In this section, we show that for any far case client
c
of type
\mathsf{A}
or
\mathsf{B}
, we have

\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})\leq 2.467\,d^{*}(c)-1.13085\,d_{1}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

We give different analysis depending on whether
f^{*}
points to
\eta_{1}
or
\eta_{2}
; this is different from our type
\mathsf{E}
analysis, where our bounds are the same in both cases. Formally, we partition the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
as the union of
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}
. We upper-bound
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)
by

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

(D.47)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

The probabilities
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}],\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}],\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}],\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]
are given in
Table

1
. We proceed by showing upper-bounds for the
{\delta}
values, the potential changes of client
c
on the worst-case swap set
\mathcal{P}
, for far clients of type
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
in the following subsections.

D.1.1
Far clients of type
\mathsf{A}
:
f_{1}=\eta_{1}

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

Type
\mathsf{A}
clients have
f_{1}=\eta_{1}
, which is the same as
\tau(f^{*})
, so we have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
in
\mathcal{P}
by implication (ii) of amenability. On that swap, the client can be served by
f^{*}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}

Since
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}\neq f_{1}
, we know
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
by implication (Siv) of amenability. On swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
c
can be served by both
f^{*}
and
f_{1}
. On swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
c
can be served by
\eta_{2}
(by implication (Siii) of amenability).
Note that
d(c,\eta_{2})\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1})
by (
3
). Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+(1+\alpha\beta)(d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1}))-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=\boxed{((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,d^{*}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Tree swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

We have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
by implication (ii) of amenability. On that swap, the client can be served by
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Tree swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}

If
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, then we have the same bound as above:

(D.48)

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}.

If
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, then on swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
c
can be served by
\eta_{2}
and
\pi(f_{1})
, by implications (ii) and (Tii) of amenability. We already showed
d(c,\eta_{2})\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1})
. We also have
d(c,\pi(f_{1}))\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
by (
2
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle~~~+(d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1}))+\beta(2d_{1}+d^{*})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(D.49)

\displaystyle=(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+2\alpha\beta-2\beta-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}.

For our choice of
\alpha,\beta
, (
D.49
) is larger than (
D.48
), so we have

\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+2\alpha\beta-2\beta-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}}.

Summarizing, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle((1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,d^{*}-((1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\quad=(1.8+1.6/\rho)\,d^{*}-(2.2-1.6/\rho)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+2\alpha\beta-2\beta-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=(1.4+{1}/{\rho})\,d^{*}-(1.8-{1}/{\rho})\,d_{1}

We now combine these inequalities using (
D.47
). If
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

If
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=0,\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}\cdot(1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1})+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot((1.4+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})d^{*}-(1.8-\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})d_{1})+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{1.925\,d^{*}-1.275\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

If
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho,\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho)\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+(\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(3+0.2\rho-0.95/\rho)\,d^{*}-(0.6\rho+0.95/\rho-0.4)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(3+0.2-0.95)\,d^{*}-(0.6+0.95-0.4)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.25\,d^{*}-1.15\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

D.1.2
Far clients of type
\mathsf{B}
:
f_{1}=\eta_{2}

When
c
is a far client of type
\mathsf{B}
, we have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
on
\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}
. This is exactly the situation for type
\mathsf{A}
clients on
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, we have the same bound for all of these cases:

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}},

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}}.

We continue to bound
{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and
{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
.

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

By implication (Siv) of amenability, we have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
.
On swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
f^{*}
and
f_{1}
. On swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
\eta_{1}
(by implication (Siii) of amenability). Also,
d(c,\eta_{1})\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
by (
3
). Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+(1+\alpha\beta)(d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1}))-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=\boxed{((1+\rho)(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,d^{*}-((1-\rho)(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}}.

Tree swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

If
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, then we have

(D.50)

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}.

If
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, then on swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
c
can be served by
\eta_{1}
and
\pi(f_{1})
by implications (ii) and (Tii) of amenability. We showed
d(c,\eta_{1})\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
. We also have
d(c,\pi(f_{1}))\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
by (
2
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}}{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)

\displaystyle\leq d_{1}+\beta\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle~~~+(d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1}))+\beta(2d_{1}+d^{*})-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

(D.51)

\displaystyle=(1+\rho+2\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+2\alpha\beta-2\beta-\rho)\,d_{1}.

Taking the maximum of (
D.50
) and (
D.51
) using
\alpha=3,\beta=0.2
, we have

\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(1.4+\max\{\rho,0.2\})\,d^{*}-(1.8-\max\{\rho,0.2\})\,d_{1}}.

Summarizing, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq((1+\rho)(1+\alpha\beta)+\beta)\,d^{*}-((1-\rho)(1+\alpha\beta)+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=(1.8+1.6\rho)\,d^{*}-(2.2-1.6\rho)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1.4+\max\{\rho,0.2\})\,d^{*}-(1.8-\max\{\rho,0.2\})\,d_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}

We now combine these inequalities using (
D.47
). If
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot((1.8+1.6\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})d^{*}-(2.2-1.6\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})d_{1})

\displaystyle~~~+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot((1.4+\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})d^{*}-(1.8-\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})d_{1})

\displaystyle~~~+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.46667d^{*}-1.13333d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

If
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=0,\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot((1.8+1.6\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})d^{*}-(2.2-1.6\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})d_{1})

\displaystyle~~~+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot((1.4+\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})d^{*}-(1.8-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})d_{1})

\displaystyle~~~+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot(1.6d^{*}-1.6d_{1})

\displaystyle~~~+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.4375d^{*}-1.1625d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

If
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho,\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho)\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+(\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+2.05\rho-1.6\rho^{2})d^{*}-(2.4-2.85\rho+1.6\rho^{2})d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+2.05\cdot\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}-1.6\cdot(\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})^{2})d^{*}-(2.4-2.85\cdot\nicefrac{{2.85}}{{3.2}}+1.6\cdot(\nicefrac{{2.85}}{{3.2}})^{2})d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.4375d^{*}-1.13085d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

D.2
Proof of (
6.18
): Close Clients of Type
\mathsf{A}

In this section, we show that for any close case client
c
with type
\mathsf{A}
, we have

\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({c})\leq 2.375\,d^{*}(c)-0.9\,d_{1}(c).

D.2.1
Clients with
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}

We first consider the case where
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
. Our analysis for this case is very simple: we directly use
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\Pr[\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})
without considering sub-events of
\mathcal{A}
.

Let us fix a generic swap set
\mathcal{P}
generated on the amenable event
\mathcal{A}
.

\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
implies that
\tau(f^{*})
always equals to
\eta_{1}=f_{1}
. By implication (ii) of amenability, we have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
in
\mathcal{P}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle+(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}.

Note that this bound also holds when
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, because our bound for
{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
does not require
f_{2}
to remain open after the swap and
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
is non-negative.

If
d_{1}\leq d^{*}
, then we have

(
d_{1}\leq d^{*}
)

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta+\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}

(
d_{2}\geq d_{1}
)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta+3\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\boxed{1.8\,d^{*}-d_{1}}.

If
d_{1}\geq d^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}

(averaging
(
3
) with
d_{2}\geq d_{1}
)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\left(\frac{1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}}{2}d_{1}-\frac{1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}}{2}d^{*}\right)

(
\rho\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
and
d^{*}\leq d_{1}
)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\left(\frac{5}{4}d_{1}-\frac{5}{4}d^{*}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+2.5\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta+2.5\beta)\,d_{1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\boxed{2.1\,d^{*}-0.9\,d_{1}}.

D.2.2
Clients with
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}

Now we turn to close clients of type
\mathsf{A}
with
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
. Our analysis for simple swaps adopts the usual strategy:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

However, we will be a little more careful in our tree swaps analysis. We further partition the tree events
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
as
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{T}_{12}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}=\mathcal{T}_{21}\cup\mathcal{T}_{22}
in the following way.

\mathcal{T}_{11}
is defined as the intersection of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and the event that
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
is the only
swap closing any facility in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
.

\mathcal{T}_{21}
is defined as the intersection of
\mathcal{T}_{2}

and the event that there is a swap which closes both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
but does not open the original copy of
f^{*}
.
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and
\mathcal{T}_{22}
are defined accordingly:
\mathcal{T}_{12}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\backslash\mathcal{T}_{11}
and
\mathcal{T}_{22}=\mathcal{T}_{2}\backslash\mathcal{T}_{21}
.

Recall that
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
implies
\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. The naive way to bound
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
is by the following:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot\max\{{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c),{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot\max\{{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c),{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

If we ignore the
O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})
term, the above bound is equal to
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
times the maximum of all four sums:
{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c),{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c),{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c),{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
. However, by relating the probabilities of
\mathcal{T}_{11}
and
\mathcal{T}_{21}
, we have the following lemma (proved in
Section

E.4
),
which gives an improved bound by not taking
{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
into the maximum.

Lemma D.1

(Type
\mathsf{A}
averaging)
.

For a close client of type
\mathsf{A}
with
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have

{\Delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot\max\{{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}\}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

We now proceed to show upper bounds for the worst-case potential change on each event.

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

We have
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
by implications (ii) and (Siv) of amenability. On swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
f^{*}
, and on
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
f_{1}
(at distance
d_{1}
) and
\eta_{2}
(at distance
\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1})
), by implication (Siii) of amenability. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle~~~+d_{1}+\beta(d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1}))-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta+\beta+\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{\rho}})\,d^{*}-(1-\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{\rho}})\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}}.

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}

By implications (Siii) and (Siv) of amenability, the three swaps
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
,
