. We use the first bound for
\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and the second bound for
\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq 1.3\,d^{*}-1.3\,d_{1}+0.6\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(1.9+0.6\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.3-0.6\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.9+0.6\cdot\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d^{*}-(1.3-0.6\cdot\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.3\,d^{*}-0.9\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

When
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=0,\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. We use the first bound for both
\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and
\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.55+0.05\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.25-0.05\rho)\,d_{1}+0.4\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(1.95+0.45\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.25-0.45\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.95+0.45\cdot\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d^{*}-(1.35-0.45\cdot\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.2875\,d^{*}-0.9125\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

When
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho,\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. We use the second bound for
\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and the first bound for
\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho)\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+(\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.982-0.2156\rho)\,d^{*}-(2.044-1.0052\rho)\,d_{1}+(1.15-\rho)\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(3.132-0.0656\rho-\rho^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.044-2.1552\rho+\rho^{2})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(3.132-0.0656\cdot\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.044-2.1552+1^{2})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.5203\,d^{*}-0.8888\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

D.5.4
d(c,h)\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
or
d(c,h)\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}+\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}(d^{*}+d_{1})
in simple swaps

Similarly to
Section

D.4.4
, our bound for
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)
in the previous case remains valid in this case.

D.5.5
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
closes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
on

\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}

If
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
, we get the same bounds as before:

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}},

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta+\rho\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\rho\beta)\,d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}}.

We proceed to bound
{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
and
{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)
.

Simple swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

Implications (ii) and (Siv) of amenability implies
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}={\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}\neq{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
. On swap
{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
f^{*}
and
f_{1}
. On swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, the client can be served by
f_{2}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq{}

\displaystyle d^{*}+\beta\,d_{1}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\displaystyle+(1+\alpha\beta)\,d_{2}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\boxed{d^{*}-(2-\beta)\,d_{1}+(1+\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{2}}.

Tree swaps with
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}

On
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}
, we assumed that
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
closes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}}.

Summarizing, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle d^{*}-(2-\beta)\,d_{1}+(1+\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\quad=d^{*}-1.8\,d_{1}+1.2\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\quad=1.6\,d^{*}-0.4\,d_{1}-0.4\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\quad=1.6\,d^{*}-d_{1}-0.2\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+\rho\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\rho\beta)\,d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\quad=(1.6+0.2\rho)\,d^{*}-(1-0.2\rho)\,d_{1}-0.2\,d_{2}

We now combine these inequalities to get an upper bound for
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)
.

When
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq 1.3\,d^{*}-1.4\,d_{1}+0.5\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+0.5\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.4-0.5\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+0.5\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d^{*}-(1.4-0.5\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.13334\,d^{*}-1.06666\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

When
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=0,\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.3+0.05\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.4-0.05\rho)\,d_{1}+0.5\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+0.55\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.4-0.55\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.8+0.55\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d^{*}-(1.4-0.55\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.2125\,d^{*}-0.9875\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

When
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho,\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho)\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+(\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(0.85+0.65\rho)\,d^{*}-(2.45-1.45\rho)\,d_{1}+(1.7-1.6\rho)\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(2.55+0.75\rho-1.6\rho^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.45-3.15\rho+1.6\rho^{2})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(2.55+0.75\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}-1.6\times(\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.45-3.15\times\nicefrac{{3.15}}{{3.2}}+1.6\times(\nicefrac{{3.15}}{{3.2}})^{2})\,d_{1}

\displaystyle~~~+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.2125\,d^{*}-0.89960\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

D.5.6
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
closes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
on

\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}

Bounds for simple swaps remain the same as before:

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{d^{*}-(2-\beta)\,d_{1}+(1+\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{2}},

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}}.

For tree swaps, we partition
\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}
as the union of
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
. On the first two events, our bounds are the same as in
Section

D.5.3
:

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta+\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-2\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-2\beta)\,d_{2}},

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta+\rho\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\rho\beta)\,d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}}.

On
\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
, we assumed that
{\langle\!\langle f^{*}\rangle\!\rangle}
closes
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Therefore,

(
{\delta}_{{\langle\!\langle f^{*},\neg f_{1},\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}}
)

{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}\leq\boxed{(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}}.

Summarizing, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle d^{*}-(2-\beta)\,d_{1}+(1+\alpha\beta-2\beta)\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=d^{*}-1.8\,d_{1}+1.2\,d_{2}

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\alpha\beta)\,d_{1}-2\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-0.4\,d_{1}-0.4\,d_{2}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+\beta)\,d^{*}-(2-2\beta)\,d_{1}+(1-2\beta)\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=1.8\,d^{*}-1.6\,d_{1}+0.6\,d_{2}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta+\rho\beta)\,d^{*}-(1-\rho\beta)\,d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=(1.6+0.2\rho)\,d^{*}-(1-0.2\rho)\,d_{1}-0.2\,d_{2}

\displaystyle{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)\leq

\displaystyle(1+\alpha\beta)\,d^{*}-d_{1}-\beta\,d_{2}

\displaystyle=1.6\,d^{*}-d_{1}-0.2\,d_{2}

We now combine these inequalities to get an upper bound for
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)
.

When
\rho(f^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq 1.35\,d^{*}-1.55\,d_{1}+0.7\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(2.05+0.7\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.55-0.7\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(2.05+0.7\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d^{*}-(1.55-0.7\times\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{2.51667\,d^{*}-1.08333\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

When
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}<\rho\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=0,\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.325+0.025\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.475-0.025\rho)\,d_{1}+0.6\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(1.925+0.625\rho)\,d^{*}-(1.475-0.625\rho)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1.925+0.625\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d^{*}-(1.475-0.625\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.39375\,d^{*}-1.00625\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

When
\rho>\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{1}]=\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho,\Pr[\mathcal{S}_{2}]=\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}},\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{5}}{{4}}-\rho)\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+(\rho-\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{S}_{2}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cap\mathcal{T}_{3-b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle~~~+\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(0.875+0.625\rho)\,d^{*}-(2.525-1.425\rho)\,d_{1}+(1.8-1.6\rho)\,d_{2}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
d_{2}\leq d^{*}+\rho(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq(2.675+0.825\rho-1.6\rho^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.525-3.225\rho+1.6\rho^{2})\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(2.675+0.825\times\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}-1.6\times(\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})^{2})\,d^{*}-(2.525-3.225+1.6)\,d_{1}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\boxed{2.39375\,d^{*}-0.9\,d_{1}}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

Appendix E
Omitted Proofs

E.1
Proof of
3.3
: There are enough local candidates

See
3.3

Proof.

Let
F_{h}
be the set of heavy local facilities,

F_{p}\subseteq F\setminus F_{h}
be the set of local facilities pointed to by at
least one optimal facility with no heavy local neighbor, and
F_{c}
be the
remaining local facilities, which are exactly the local candidates.
|F_{h}|+|F_{p}|+|F_{c}|=

the number of local facilities, which in turn is at least the number of
optimal facilities. There are at least
(t_{\mathsf{d}}+2)|F_{h}|/2

many optimal facilities having a heavy local neighbor because 1) a heavy local facility is a neighbor of at least
t_{\mathsf{d}}+2
optimal facilities, and 2) each optimal facility has at most
2
local neighbors. Finally, each local facility in
F_{p}
is pointed to by an optimal facility with no heavy local neighbor, so the total
number of optimal facilities is at least
(t_{\mathsf{d}}+2)|F_{h}|/2+|F_{p}|
. In other
words,
|F_{c}|\geq\frac{t_{\mathsf{d}}}{2}|F_{h}|
.

∎

E.2
Proof of
3.5
: Balancing Procedure

See
3.5

Proof of
3.5
.

Recall
|G|=|R|+r
, where
r\geq\frac{16x^{5}\theta^{2}(\theta+1)}{\varepsilon}

suffices.
For each integer
s\in\{-x,\ldots,x\}
let
D_{s}
be the sets
S

with discrepancy
|S\cap G|-|S\cap R|
. Each set in
D_{0}
can
be output immediately. If for some
i,j
we have
|D_{i}|\geq j/\varepsilon
and

|D_{-j}|\geq i/\varepsilon
, and there is no edge in
H
, then we
can choose some
j
sets uniformly at random from
D_{i}
,
and
i
sets from
D_{-j}
, and merge these together.

However, since there are forbidden sets (a set
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are forbidden
if there is an edge between them in
H
), we need one more
ingredient. We claim that if some
D_{i},D_{-j}
have

\geq 8x^{2}\theta
sets, then we can find
j
sets from
D_{i}
and

i
sets from
D_{-j}
that are not forbidden for each
other. Indeed, pick a random collection of
j
sets from
D_{i}
and

i
sets from
D_{-j}
. The probability that any one set has an edge
to any of the other
i+j-1
sets is

\leq\frac{(i+j-1)\theta}{8x^{2}\theta}<\frac{1}{4x}
. Hence, a
union bound over all the
i+j
sets says that with probability at
least a half, this collection does not have any edges of
H
within
it, and hence we can merge this collection together.

However, above procedure does not ensure two sets are combined with
probability at most
\varepsilon
. To do so, if we find some pair

D_{i},D_{-j}
with
\geq\frac{8x^{2}\theta}{\varepsilon}
sets, then we can randomly
partition each of
D_{i}
and
D_{-j}
into
1/\varepsilon
equal-sized subgroups
with
8x^{2}\theta
sets each. Now we can merge some
j
sets from any
subgroup from
D_{i}
with some
i
sets from a randomly chosen
subgroup of
D_{-j}
to form a set with equal number of greens and
reds, exactly as above. Henceforth, we assume that for each

D_{i},D_{-j}
, at least one has fewer than
\frac{8x^{2}\theta}{\varepsilon}
sets.

Finally, since the greens outnumber the reds by
r
, we know there
exists a value
j>0
such that

|D_{j}|\geq r/x={16x^{4}\theta^{2}(\theta+1)}/\varepsilon
. Thus, we know each

D_{s}
with
s<0
has at most
\frac{8x^{2}\theta}{\varepsilon}
sets each. We randomly
divide
D_{j}
into
\frac{16x^{3}\theta^{2}}{\varepsilon}
parts of of size
x(\theta+1)

sets each. Note any two sets
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
fall in the same part
with probability at most

\frac{\varepsilon}{16x^{3}\theta^{2}}\leq\varepsilon
. From each part
pick
x
sets that have no edge in
H
between themselves and call them a
positive group; this can be done because the maximum degree of
H

is at most
\theta
. Each such positive group has at least
x
extra
green points. On the other hand, there are at most
x\cdot\frac{8x^{2}\theta}{\varepsilon}=\frac{8x^{3}\theta}{\varepsilon}

negative sets, i.e., in
\{D_{i}\}_{i<0}
. Each negative set has
edges to at most
\theta
sets, so there are at most
\frac{8x^{3}\theta^{2}}{\varepsilon}

sets with an edge to some negative set. Since there are

\frac{16x^{3}\theta^{2}}{\varepsilon}
positive groups, there are at least

\frac{8x^{3}\theta^{2}}{\varepsilon}
positive groups with no edge to any negative set, so
we can merge each negative set with a randomly-chosen such positive
group. This ensures that each new set has more green points than
red, and two sets are combined with probability at most

\frac{\varepsilon}{8x^{3}\theta^{2}}\leq\varepsilon
. The newly-created sets
have of size at most
O(x^{2})
. Finally, each remaining set can form
a group by itself, because they have more green points.
∎

E.3
Proof of
5.3
: Crude Upper Bound of Potential Change

See
5.3

Proof.

Since every local facility is closed by at most 3 swaps in
\mathcal{P}
, there are at most 6 swaps in
\mathcal{P}
that closes any facility in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
{\delta}_{(P,Q)}(c)\leq O(d^{*}+d_{1})
for these 6 swaps
(P,Q)
.

If
f^{*}
has a heavy local neighbor
h
, the client can be served by
h
at distance
\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}(d^{*}+d_{1})
. We assume henceforth that
f^{*}
has no heavy local neighbor, which means
\tau(f^{*})
is not heavy and never closed as a local surrogate.

When
\mathcal{P}
is a simple swap set, the client can be served by either
f^{*}
(at distance
\leq d^{*}
) or

\tau(f^{*})
(at distance
\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}(d^{*}+d_{1})
).
When
\mathcal{P}
is a tree swap set, we show that one of the following facilities must be open after every swap in
\mathcal{P}
:

\displaystyle f^{*}~

\displaystyle\textup{at distance}~\leq d^{*},

\displaystyle\tau(f^{*})~

\displaystyle\textup{at distance}~\leq d^{*}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}(d^{*}+d_{1}),

\displaystyle\pi(\tau(f^{*}))~

\displaystyle\textup{at distance}~\leq d^{*}+2\cdot\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}(d^{*}+d_{1}).

It suffices to show that any swap closing
\tau(f^{*})
must open either
f^{*}
or
\pi(\tau(f^{*}))
.
If
\tau(f^{*})
is closed as an optimal surrogate,
\pi(\tau(f^{*}))
must be open because edges on short cycles are not deleted in the edge deletion step (
Corollary

3.8
). We thus focus on the swap closing the original copy of
\tau(f^{*})
henceforth.

Consider the 1-forest
G_{1}
before edge deletion. The edges in
G_{1}
from
f^{*}
to
\tau(f^{*})
and from
\tau(f^{*})
to
\pi(\tau(f^{*}))
cannot both be deleted in the edge deletion step, because we always choose
t_{\mathsf{h}}
as an even number and
G_{1}
is bipartite (when self-loops are ignored). Therefore, either
f^{*}
or
\pi(\tau(f^{*}))
must be in the same swap with
\tau(f^{*})
, as desired.
∎

E.4
Proof of
Lemma

D.1
: Combining Type
\mathsf{A}
Inequalities

See
D.1

We first prove
Lemma

D.1
assuming the following lemma, which we prove later.

Lemma E.1
.

For a close client of type
\mathsf{A}
with
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we have

\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}]\leq\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}]+O(\varepsilon).

Proof of
Lemma

D.1
.

Define
p_{ij}:=\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{ij}]
. Note that
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
implies that
p_{11}+p_{12}=p_{21}+p_{22}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Define
p_{\Delta}:=p_{11}-p_{21}=p_{22}-p_{12}
.
Lemma

E.1
implies
p_{\Delta}\geq-O(\varepsilon)
. Define
{\delta}_{\max}:=\max\{{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}\}
.
5.3
implies
{\delta}_{\max}\leq O(d^{*}+d_{1})
.

Lemma

D.1
is proved by the following chain of inequalities:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle~~~+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}]{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle\leq p_{11}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{12}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle~~~+p_{21}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{22}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

(
5.4
and
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)\geq-10d_{1}
)

\displaystyle~~~+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=p_{21}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{\Delta}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{12}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{12}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle~~~+p_{21}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{21}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{\Delta}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+p_{12}{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)

\displaystyle~~~+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle\leq p_{21}{\delta}_{\max}+p_{\Delta}({\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{11}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c)+{\delta}_{\mathcal{T}_{22}\cap\mathcal{A}}(c))+p_{12}{\delta}_{\max}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

(
p_{\Delta}\geq-O(\varepsilon)
,
{\delta}_{\mathcal{E}}(c)\geq-10d_{1}
and
{\delta}_{\max}\leq O(d^{*}+d_{1})
)

\displaystyle\leq p_{21}{\delta}_{\max}+p_{\Delta}{\delta}_{\max}+p_{12}{\delta}_{\max}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=(p_{21}+p_{\Delta}+p_{12}){\delta}_{\max}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1})

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}\cdot{\delta}_{\max}+O(\varepsilon)(d^{*}+d_{1}).

∎

We now turn to proving
Lemma

E.1
. Before doing so, we need some deeper understandings of the edge deletion procedure, which we establish in
Section

E.4.1
. The proof of
Lemma

E.1
is presented in
Section

E.4.2
.

E.4.1
Probability of Surviving Edge Deletion

Let
T
be a 1-tree in the 1-forest
G_{1}
before the edge deletion procedure. The edge deletion procedure splits
T
into several connected components by deleting some edges from
T
. In this section, we prove upper and lower bounds on the probabilities that paths in
T
remain connected after edge deletion.

Let
\ell>0
denote the cycle length of
T
. Condition on the height threshold
t_{\mathsf{h}}
being fixed. We prove the following two lemmas:

Lemma E.2

(Upper bound)
.

Suppose
p
is a directed simple path in
T
of length
s
. If
\ell\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, then the probability that no edge in
p
is deleted is at most
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}(1+t_{\mathsf{h}}/\ell)
. If
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, and we further assume that
p
doesn’t contain any cycle edge, then the probability is exactly
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}
.

Proof.

If
s\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, the lemma is trivial because any path after edge deletion has length at most
t_{\mathsf{h}}-1
. We assume
s<t_{\mathsf{h}}
henceforth.

Suppose vertices on
p
are
v_{0}\leftarrow v_{1}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow v_{s}
. We first consider the case where
\ell\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
. We prove that as long as the (unique) simple path
p^{*}
from
v_{0}
to
r
has length equal to
-1,-2,\cdots,-s
modulo
t_{\mathsf{h}}
, some edge on path
p
is deleted. Indeed, suppose
p^{*}
has length
-i
modulo
t_{\mathsf{h}}
. If
p^{*}
doesn’t contain any vertex in
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{s}\}
, then the edge out of
v_{i}
is deleted by
3.7
. Otherwise,
r
must be one of
v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{s}
, in which case the edge out of
r
is deleted.

Suppose
\ell=ut_{\mathsf{h}}+w
for
u,w\in\mathbb{Z}
where
0\leq w<t_{\mathsf{h}}
. There are at most
(t_{\mathsf{h}}-s)(u+1)
choices of
r
such that
p^{*}
has length not in
\{-1,\cdots,-s\}
modulo
t_{\mathsf{h}}
. Therefore, when
\ell\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, the probability that no edge in
p
is deleted is at most
(t_{\mathsf{h}}-s)(u+1)/\ell=\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot(\frac{ut_{\mathsf{h}}}{\ell}+\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}}{\ell})\leq\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot(1+t_{\mathsf{h}}/\ell)
.

When
\ell\leq t_{h}
and
p
doesn’t contain a cycle edge, an edge on the path
p
is deleted if and only if
p^{*}
has length
-1,-2,\cdots,-s
modulo
t_{\mathsf{h}}
by
3.7
. Since the cycle length is exactly
t_{\mathsf{h}}
after dummy vertices are inserted on it, the probability that no edge on
p
is deleted is exactly
\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}
.
∎

Lemma E.3

(Lower bound)
.

Let
v_{1},v_{2},v^{*}
be vertices in
T
and
p_{1},p_{2}
be directed simple paths in
T
from
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
to
v^{*}
, respectively. Suppose both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have lengths no greater than
s
. If
\ell\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, then the probability that no edge on either path
p_{1},p_{2}
is deleted is at least
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}(1-2t_{\mathsf{h}}/\ell)
. If
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, and we further assume that
v_{1}
is on the cycle, then the probability is at least
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}
.

Proof.

Again, the lemma is trivial if
s\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
. Assume
s<t_{\mathsf{h}}
henceforth.

Let us first consider the case where
\ell\geq t_{\mathsf{h}}
. Consider the vertices on the cycle that are different from
v^{*}
but have paths to
v^{*}
with length at most
s
. There are at most
s
such vertices, and they form a contiguous part of the cycle. If
r
is not among these vertices, then the simple path
p^{*}
from
v^{*}
to
r
contains no vertex on
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
except
v^{*}
itself. If we further assume that
p^{*}
has length not in
-1,-2,\cdots,-s
modulo
t_{\mathsf{h}}
, then by
3.7
no edge on either path
p_{1},p_{2}
is deleted. Therefore, assuming
\ell-s=ut_{\mathsf{h}}+w
for
u,w\in\mathbb{Z}
where
0\leq w<t_{\mathsf{h}}
, the probability that no edge on either path is deleted is at least
u(t_{\mathsf{h}}-s)/\ell=\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\frac{ut_{\mathsf{h}}}{\ell}=\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot(1-\frac{s+w}{\ell})\geq\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot(1-2t_{\mathsf{h}}/\ell)
.

When
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
and
v_{1}
is on the cycle, every edge on
p_{1}
must be on the cycle. Since no edge on the cycle is deleted by our convention, the probability that no edge on either path is deleted is lower bounded by the probability that no edge on the shortest path
p^{\prime}
from
v_{2}
to the cycle is deleted.
p^{\prime}
is a part of
p_{2}
, so
p^{\prime}
has length at most
s
. By the second part of the previous lemma, the probability that no edge on
p^{\prime}
is deleted is at least
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}
.
∎

E.4.2
Proof of
Lemma

E.1

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

E.1
. Define
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as the union of the defiant event
\mathcal{D}
(
Definition

5.1
) and the following events:

(i)

\mathcal{P}
is a tree swap set, and, before edge deletion, the cycle in the 1-tree containing the original copy of
f^{*}
has length
\ell
in the range
(t_{\mathsf{h}},\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil\cdot t_{\mathsf{h}})
;

(ii)

\mathcal{P}
is a tree swap set, and two connected components each containing a facility in
\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
are combined in the balancing procedure.

Event (i) happens with probability
O(\varepsilon)
because our height threshold
t_{\mathsf{h}}
is chosen uniformly at random from
2\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil,2\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil^{2},\cdots,2\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil^{\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil}
. Event (ii) happens with probability
O(\varepsilon)
as well due to
3.6
and
3.5
. By a union bound with
5.4
, we have

Claim E.4
.

The event
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
happens with probability
O(\varepsilon)
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.1
.

If either
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
is heavy, then
\mathcal{T}_{21}
never happens. Indeed,
\mathcal{T}_{21}
assumes the existence of a swap closing both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, but heavy local facilities are never closed. Hence, we assume neither
f_{1}
nor
f_{2}
is heavy.

By
E.4
and the union bound, it suffices to prove
\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]
. By law of total probability, it suffices to prove

(E.54)

\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}_{i}]\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}_{i}]

for a partition
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{t}
of the entire probability space.

If
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{S}
, then both sides of (
E.54
) become zero. Let us condition on the tree event
\mathcal{T}
henceforth. Conditioned on
\mathcal{T}
, the probabilities of
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}
and
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
are both
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
since
\rho(f^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
. Note that the set of heavy local/optimal facilities doesn’t depend on the random function
\tau
. Therefore, if we condition on the
\tau
’s of all optimal facilities except
f^{*}
, the out-edges of the original copies of all facilities in
G_{1}
except
f^{*}
are determined, where
G_{1}
is the 1-forest after degree reduction but before edge deletion. Let
G_{1}^{*}
be
G_{1}
with the out-edge of the original copy of
f^{*}
removed. If we ignore the identity of the local and optimal surrogates, everything else in
G_{1}^{*}
is determined.
Moreover, the conditioning we did is independent of
\tau(f^{*})
, so the conditional probabilities of
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}
and
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
are both still
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

Note that
f^{*}
may be a heavy optimal facility, in which case
f^{*}
has new copies in
G_{1}
. We use
f^{*}
to refer to only the original copy.
\tau(f^{*})
may also be a heavy local facility when
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
(note that we assumed
\eta_{1}=f_{1}
is not heavy), in which case
f^{*}
points to itself in
G_{1}
. If either
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
is chosen as a surrogate, then
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
cannot happen because
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
happens. We thus assume
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
only appear as their original copies in
G_{1}
. Since
f^{*}
is the only vertex in
G_{1}^{*}
that doesn’t have an out-edge,
f^{*}
is the root of a tree, and all other connected components of
G_{1}^{*}
are 1-trees.

We divide our proof into five cases depending on the structure of
G_{1}^{*}
:

1.

f^{*},f_{1},f_{2}
are all in the different connected components;

2.

f^{*},f_{1}
are in the same tree, different from
f_{2}
;

3.

f^{*},f_{2}
are in the same tree, different from
f_{1}
;

4.

f_{1},f_{2}
are in the same 1-tree (denoted by
T
), different from
f^{*}
;

5.

all three are in the same tree (denoted by
T^{*}
).

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denote the event that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are in the same connected component in
G_{2}
, where
G_{2}
is the graph after the edge deletion procedure. Since
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
includes the case where the edge from
f^{*}
to
\eta_{1}=f_{1}
is deleted in the edge deletion step, we have
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
denote the event that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are in the same connected component in
G_{2}
but different from
f^{*}
. Since subtracting
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
rules out the possibility of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
being combined in the balancing step, we have
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{1}
.

In case 1,
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
never happens because
\mathcal{E}_{1}
never happens. Indeed,
f_{1},f_{2}
must be in different connected components in
G_{1}
and thus must be in different connected components in
G_{2}
.

In cases 2&3,
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
never happens either because
\mathcal{E}_{0}
never happens. Indeed, the only way
f_{1}
can connect to
f_{2}
(by an undirected path in
G_{1}
) is through
f^{*}
, and in the edge deletion procedure, there is no way to put
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
in the same connected component of
G_{2}
without also putting
f^{*}
in it.

In case 4,
f^{*}
is not on the cycle part of
T
, so the height threshold
t_{\mathsf{h}}
and the choice of
r\in T
in the edge deletion step are both independent of
\tau(f^{*})
. Once conditioned on
t_{\mathsf{h}},r
, whether or not
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are in the same connected component in
G_{2}
is determined. We assume that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are in the same connected component of
G_{2}
because otherwise
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
never happens. If
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}(=f_{1})
, then we know
\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
must happen, because
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}
happens. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
happens only when
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
simply because
\mathcal{T}_{21}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{2}
. Therefore, if we let
\mathcal{E}
be the event summarizing all the conditioning we did so far, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}]=

\displaystyle\Pr[\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}|\mathcal{E}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}]\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}|\mathcal{E}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},

and thus (
E.54
) holds for
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}
.

Case 5 is a little tricky since the cycle structure of
T
, the 1-tree in
G_{1}
containing all of
f^{*},f_{1},f_{2}
, may depend on where
f^{*}
points to. Condition on the height threshold
t_{\mathsf{h}}
being fixed, and let
\mathcal{E}
be the event summarizing all the conditioning we did so far. Let
f_{a}
be the least common ancestor of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in
T^{*}
, and let
s
denote the path length from
f_{i}
to
f_{a}
maximized over
i=1,2
.

Conditioned on
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}
, or equivalently
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, the probability of
\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is 1 if the cycle length
\ell
of
T
is in the range
(t_{\mathsf{h}},\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil\cdot t_{\mathsf{h}})
, and if
\ell
is not in the range, the conditional probability of
\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is at least the conditional probability of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, which is at least
\max\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\}(1-O(\varepsilon))
by
Lemma

E.3
(Observe that
f_{1}=\eta_{1}
is on the cycle of
T
because
f^{*}
points to it on event
\mathcal{T}_{1}
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{11}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr[\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{1}|\mathcal{E}]\cdot\max\Big\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\Big\}(1-O(\varepsilon))

(E.55)

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot\max\Big\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\Big\}(1-O(\varepsilon)).

On the other hand,
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
happens only when
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
. Condition on
\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}
. If the cycle length
\ell
is in the range
(t_{\mathsf{h}},\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil\cdot t_{\mathsf{h}})
, then
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
never happens. If
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
, and
f_{a}
is on the cycle, then
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
never happens either because
\mathcal{E}_{0}
never happens. Indeed, the only possible undirected path in
T
connecting
f_{1}
with
f_{2}
without passing through
f^{*}
intersects the cycle, so
f_{1},f_{2}
have to connect to the cycle after edge deletion to make
\mathcal{E}_{0}
happen, but the cycle contains
f^{*}
and remains connected after edge deletion (because
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
). Therefore, we assume either
\ell\geq\lceil\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}\rceil\cdot t_{\mathsf{h}}
, or
\ell\leq t_{\mathsf{h}}
and
f_{a}
is not on the cycle. In this case, the conditional probability of
\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is at most the conditional probability of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, which is at most
\max\Big\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\Big\}(1+O(\varepsilon))
by
Lemma

E.2
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{T}_{21}\backslash\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\tau(f^{*})=\eta_{2}|\mathcal{E}]\cdot\max\Big\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\Big\}(1+O(\varepsilon))

(E.56)

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot\max\Big\{\frac{t_{\mathsf{h}}-s}{t_{\mathsf{h}}},0\Big\}(1+O(\varepsilon)).

Combining (
E.4.2
) and (
E.4.2
), we know (
E.54
) holds for
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}
.
∎

E.5
Proof of
D.3
: Subtypes within Type
\mathsf{C}

See
D.3

Proof.

Recall that
g^{*}
is
\pi(f_{1})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{b}
is the event that
\mathcal{P}
is a simple swap and

g^{*}
points to
\eta_{b}(g^{*})
. Similarly
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{b}
is the event that
\mathcal{P}
is a tree swap and

g^{*}
points to
\eta_{b}(g^{*})
.

If either
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
is heavy, then condition (a) or (b) holds. We assume neither
f_{1}
nor
f_{2}
is heavy henceforth. In other words, the swaps
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
and
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{2}\rangle\!\rangle}
both exist.

Let
g
be the closest local facility to
g^{*}
that is different from
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
.
Intuitively, we show that either a client is close to
g
or there is a tree that contains all

f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, and
f^{*}
.

If
d(g,g^{*})\leq d(f_{1},g^{*})
, then we have
d(c,g)\leq d_{1}+d(f_{1},g^{*})+d(g,g^{*})\leq d_{1}+2d(f_{1},g^{*})\leq 3d_{1}+2d^{*}
.
Furthermore,
when we generate tree swaps,
f_{1}
points to
g^{*}=\pi(f_{1})
in the 1-forest
G_{1}
after degree reduction.
If
f_{1}
points to a new copy of
g^{*}
, we know that
f_{1}
is not among the
t_{\mathsf{d}}
closest local facilities to
g^{*}
in
\pi^{-1}(g^{*})
.
Therefore, we know
d(g,g^{*})\leq d(f_{1},g^{*})
.
Note that
g
and
f_{1}
are not closed in the same simple swap by implication (Siii’) of amenability, so condition (c) holds in this case.

We can now assume that
f_{1}
points to the original copy of
g^{*}
and
d(g,g^{*})>d(f_{1},g^{*})
. If
g^{*}=f^{*}
,
we know condition (e) holds, because both edges
f_{1}\rightarrow f^{*},f^{*}\rightarrow f_{2}
remain after the edge deletion step by amenability. We assume
g^{*}\neq f^{*}
henceforth.

If
\rho(g^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
, we know
\tau(g^{*})=\eta_{1}(g^{*})
deterministically. Moreover,
d(g,g^{*})>d(f_{1},g^{*})
implies that
\tau(g^{*})
is either
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
. If
\eta_{1}(g^{*})=f_{1}
, then
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
must open
g^{*}
by implication (ii’) of amenability, so condition (c) holds in this case since
d(c,g^{*})\leq d_{1}+d(f_{1},g^{*})\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
. Otherwise,
\eta_{1}(g^{*})=f_{2}
, and then condition (e) holds, because the edges
f_{1}\rightarrow g^{*},g^{*}\rightarrow f_{2},f^{*}\rightarrow f_{2}
all survive edge deletion by amenability, so
f_{1},f_{2},f^{*}
must all be in the same swap.

It remains to consider the case where
\rho(g^{*})>\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
. If
f_{2}=\eta_{b}(g^{*})\in\{\eta_{1}(g^{*}),\eta_{2}(g^{*})\}
, then condition (f) holds because the edges
f_{1}\rightarrow g^{*},g^{*}\rightarrow f_{2},f^{*}\rightarrow\tau(f^{*})\in\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
all survive edge deletion on
\mathcal{T}_{b}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
(see the left graph in
Figure

E.13
). Otherwise,
f_{2}\notin\{\eta_{1}(g^{*}),\eta_{2}(g^{*})\}
, and in this case we know
\eta_{1}(g^{*})=f_{1}
and
\eta_{2}(g^{*})=g
because
d(g,g^{*})>d(f_{1},g^{*})
. We show that condition (c) or (d) holds, depending on whether
\rho(g^{*})\leq\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}
. Indeed, on
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
, we know
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
opens
g^{*}
at distance
\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}
by implication (ii’) of amenability, and on
\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}
, we know either
g^{*}
or
g
is open after swap
{\langle\!\langle\neg f_{1}\rangle\!\rangle}
, again by implication (ii’) of amenability, and
d(c,g)\leq 2d_{1}+d^{*}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho(g^{*})}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1})
(see the right graph in
Figure

E.13
).
∎

f^{*}

f_{1}
g^{*}
g
f_{2}
d^{*}
d_{1}
d^{*}+d_{1}
\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}\cdot(d^{*}+d_{1})
f^{*}

f_{1}

Figure E.13:
In the figure, dashed edges represent the random function
\tau
. In the left graph, whenever
g^{*}
points to
f_{2}
,
f_{1},f_{2},f^{*}
are all in the same swap, so condition (f) holds. In the right graph, condition (d) holds.
